An assignment of functions is a map $a:v\mapsto g_v$; write its integral as $\Lambda(a)$. The two real functions on the plus-half labels that enter the cut bilinear form are $$F(x)=\prod_{v\in V_+}a(v)(x_v),
 \qquad
 G(x)=\prod_{v\in V_+}a(\theta v)(x_v).$$ Its mixed value is $\Lambda(a)$, and its two quadratic values are $\Lambda(a\circ\phi_+)$ and $\Lambda(a\circ\phi_-)$. Cauchy–Schwarz for the positive semidefinite matrix gives $$|\Lambda(a)|^2
 \le \Lambda(a\circ\phi_+)\Lambda(a\circ\phi_-),
 \qquad \Lambda(a\circ\phi_\pm)\ge0.
 \label{lift:fold-cs}$$ In particular a constant assignment has nonnegative integral, proving the first assertion of <a href="#eq:10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:10">[eq:10]</a>.

We next give a finite sequence of fold maps that sends every vertex to one vertex. In one block write $p_i$ for the position of symbol $i$ in its permutation word. Folding onto the half where $i$ precedes $i+1$ sorts the pair $(p_i,p_{i+1})$, leaving all other entries unchanged. Apply the comparisons $i=1,\ldots,L-1$ in that order, and repeat this sweep $L-1$ times. A sweep moves the largest entry to the end. Once the largest entries occupy their final positions, the next sweep leaves them there and places the largest remaining entry immediately before them. Thus every position array becomes $(1,\ldots,L)$. Perform these sweeps in each block. If their fold maps in chronological order are $\phi_1,\ldots,\phi_m$, then $$\phi_m\circ\cdots\circ\phi_1(v)=v_*
 \quad\text{for every }v\in V,$$ where $v_*$ has the identity word in every block.

Now fix a finite nonempty list of real functions and let $M$ be the maximum absolute integral over all assignments from that list, allowing repetition. If $M>0$, take a maximizing assignment. Each of its folded integrals is nonnegative and at most $M$, while their product is at least $M^2$ by <a href="#lift:fold-cs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:fold-cs">[lift:fold-cs]</a>. Both therefore equal $M$. Hence either fold preserves maximality. Apply the pullbacks in reverse order, first $\phi_m$, then $\phi_{m-1}$, and so on. The final assignment is $a\circ\phi_m\circ\cdots\circ\phi_1$, which is constant. It follows that $M$ is a diagonal value. Conversely, constant assignments are included in the maximum. We have proved that the maximum absolute mixed integral equals the largest diagonal value in the list; this is also true when $M=0$.

For $\epsilon>0$, apply this conclusion to the list $$\frac{g_v}{(Z_{l,\mathcal P}(g_v)+\epsilon)^{1/d}},
 \qquad v\in V.$$ Each diagonal value in this list is at most one. Multilinearity gives $|\mathcal Z_{l,\mathcal P}((g_v)_v)|
 \le\prod_v(Z_{l,\mathcal P}(g_v)+\epsilon)^{1/d}$. Let $\epsilon\downarrow0$. This proves the second assertion, including cases where some diagonal values vanish. ◻

</details>

Define $$\|g\|_{l,\mathcal P}=Z_{l,\mathcal P}(g)^{1/d}
 \quad\text{for real }g.$$ For $B\subseteq\mathcal P$, let $\mathbb E_B g$ mean uniform averaging over the coordinates in $B$, with all other coordinates held fixed. The resulting function may be viewed on either $X_{\mathcal P}$ or $X_{\mathcal P\setminus B}$.

<a id="stmt-lift-seminorm"></a>
<div id="lift:seminorm" class="lemma">

<a id="lift:transport"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Seminorm and changes of coordinates). *The map $g\mapsto\|g\|_{l,\mathcal P}$ is a seminorm on the real functions on $X_{\mathcal P}$. It satisfies $$|\mathbb Eg|\le\|g\|_{l,\mathcal P},
 \qquad
 \|\mathbb E_Bg\|_{l,\mathcal P\setminus B}
 =\|\mathbb E_Bg\|_{l,\mathcal P}
 \le\|g\|_{l,\mathcal P}.$$ Translations of the input are isometries. Moreover, if $D$ is a positive integer coprime to every prime of $\mathcal P$, then for any $a'\in X_{\mathcal P}$, $$\left\|g\bigl(\lambda(D)^{-1}(\,\cdot-a')\bigr)
       \right\|_{l,\mathcal P}
 =\|g\|_{lD,\mathcal P}.
 \label{lift:transport}$$ Here the affine change is interpreted separately at each prime.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lift-seminorm"><summary>Proof of stmt-lift-seminorm</summary>

*Proof.* Nonnegativity follows from Proposition <a href="#lift:signed-holder" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:signed-holder">16</a>. Since $d$ is even, homogeneity of $Z$ gives absolute homogeneity of its $d$-th root. Expanding $Z(g+h)$ by its slots and applying <a href="#eq:10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:10">[eq:10]</a> termwise gives $$Z_{l,\mathcal P}(g+h)
 \le\sum_{S\subseteq V}\|g\|_{l,\mathcal P}^{|S|}
                         \|h\|_{l,\mathcal P}^{d-|S|}
 =\bigl(\|g\|_{l,\mathcal P}+\|h\|_{l,\mathcal P}\bigr)^d.$$ Taking roots proves the triangle inequality. Putting $g$ in one slot and 1 in all others in <a href="#eq:10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:10">[eq:10]</a> proves mean domination, since the single-slot marginal is uniform and $Z(1)=1$.

The simultaneous translation invariance of the tuple laws proves that translations are isometries. Let $G_B\subseteq X_{\mathcal P}$ be the additive subgroup of vectors supported on $B$. Then $$(\mathbb E_Bg)(z)=\frac1{|G_B|}\sum_{a\in G_B}g(z+a).$$ The triangle inequality gives the asserted contraction. Since $\mathbb E_Bg$ is independent of $B$, the probability laws at those primes integrate out, proving the equality of the two seminorms. Finally, $\lambda(D)$ is a unit at each remaining prime, and <a href="#eq:7" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:7">[eq:7]</a> pushes the product law at $l$ exactly to that at $lD$. This proves <a href="#lift:transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:transport">[lift:transport]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Decay at large Fourier denominators

Conditional mixing gives a different estimate, valid also after removing the exceptional part of each prime law. Its proof requires orthogonality between different exact supports; applying the triangle inequality to all Fourier coefficients would lose this decay.

<a id="stmt-lift-large-denominator"></a>
<div id="lift:large-denominator" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:11"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Large-denominator estimate). *Fix $l\ge1$ and a finite prime set $\mathcal P$ as above. At each prime choose either $\rho_p=\mu_{l,p}$ or $\rho_p=\mu_{l,p}^{\circ}$. Let $g_v:X_{\mathcal P}\to\mathbb C$ be arbitrary functions. If, in one distinguished slot $v$, every Fourier coefficient at a denominator at most $R$ vanishes, where $R\ge1$, then $$\left|\int\prod_{u\in V}g_u(z_u)\,
                  d\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P}\rho_p\right|
 \le R^{-\gamma}\|g_v\|_2
                     \prod_{u\ne v}\|g_u\|_{2(d-1)}.
 \label{eq:11}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lift-large-denominator"><summary>Proof of stmt-lift-large-denominator</summary>

*Proof.* At one prime, let $\mathfrak m_p$ denote the marginal law of the labels in all slots other than $v$, and let $$T_p f=\mathbb E_{\rho_p}\bigl(f(z_v)\mid(z_u)_{u\ne v}\bigr).$$ This is an operator from uniform $L^2(\mathbb F_p)$ to $L^2(\mathfrak m_p)$. It preserves constants. It sends uniform-mean-zero inputs to $\mathfrak m_p$-mean-zero outputs, since $\mathbb E_{\mathfrak m_p}T_pf=\mathbb Ef$. By <a href="#eq:8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:8">[eq:8]</a>, its norm on the mean-zero subspace is at most $p^{-\gamma}$.

The product law makes the conditional operator for all primes equal to $T=\bigotimes_pT_p$: this identity holds first for products of one-prime functions, by independence across primes, and then for every function by linearity. The exact-support $B$ Fourier space has a mean-zero factor at primes in $B$ and a constant factor elsewhere. On this space the operator norm is at most $q_B^{-\gamma}$. Furthermore, the images of two distinct exact-support spaces are orthogonal in $L^2(\bigotimes_p\mathfrak m_p)$. At a prime in the symmetric difference of the supports, one image factor is constant and the other has mean zero. Their inner product is zero, and tensoring preserves this orthogonality.

Decompose $g_v=\sum_{q_B>R}g_{v,B}$ by exact supports. Both the domain summands and their images are orthogonal, so $$\|Tg_v\|_2^2
 =\sum_{q_B>R}\|Tg_{v,B}\|_2^2
 \le\sum_{q_B>R}q_B^{-2\gamma}\|g_{v,B}\|_2^2
 \le R^{-2\gamma}\|g_v\|_2^2.$$ Condition the original integral on the other slots and apply Cauchy–Schwarz. The remaining factor is bounded by $$\left\|\prod_{u\ne v}g_u(z_u)\right\|_2
 \le\prod_{u\ne v}
       \left(\mathbb E|g_u(z_u)|^{2(d-1)}\right)^{1/(2(d-1))}
 =\prod_{u\ne v}\|g_u\|_{2(d-1)},$$ using ordinary Hölder and the uniform single-slot marginals. This proves <a href="#eq:11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:11">[eq:11]</a>. If $\mathcal P$ is empty, the frequency hypothesis forces $g_v=0$, and the conclusion is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Fourier lifts and uniform moments

A bounded function on an integer interval gives Fourier coefficients at rational frequencies. We transfer a finite set of these coefficients to $X_{\mathcal P}$. Such a transfer need not preserve pointwise bounds, so we prove bounds for every fixed moment, following the Fourier-lift argument of (OpenAI 2026, Lemmas 5.1–5.2) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[squarepower](#ref-squarepower)]. We must also allow arbitrary subfamilies of complete exact supports, because fixing some residue coordinates changes which denominators remain.

Let $I$ be a nonempty consecutive interval of integers and let $f:I\to\mathbb C$. Put $$\widehat f_I(\xi)=\frac1{|I|}\sum_{x\in I}f(x)e(-x\xi).$$ For $B\subseteq\mathcal P$, define its exact-support contribution $$f_B(z)=\sum_{q(\xi)=q_B}\widehat f_I(\xi)e(z\xi),$$ where the frequencies are characters of $X_{\mathcal P}$. For $Q\ge1$, the Fourier lift is $$\mathcal L_{I,\mathcal P,Q}f(z)
 =\sum_{\substack{B\subseteq\mathcal P\\q_B\le Q}}f_B(z)
 =\sum_{\substack{\xi\text{ on }X_{\mathcal P}\\q(\xi)\le Q}}
                   \widehat f_I(\xi)e(z\xi).$$ Each $f_B$ depends only on the coordinates in $B$, and has mean zero in each of those coordinates. If $f$ is real, all these functions are real because each summation set is closed under negation. Retaining a support $B$ always means retaining all its frequencies.

<a id="stmt-lift-moments"></a>
<div id="lift:moments" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:12"></a>
**Proposition 19** (Uniform lift moments). *Let $\mathcal P$ be any finite set of primes, $Q\ge1$, and let $I$ be a consecutive integer interval with $|I|\ge10Q^6$. For every integer $r\ge1$, every $\epsilon>0$, every function $f:I\to\mathbb C$, and every subfamily $\mathcal F\subseteq\{B\subseteq\mathcal P:q_B\le Q\}$, $$\left\|\sum_{B\in\mathcal F}f_B\right\|_{2r}
 \le C_{r,\epsilon}\|f\|_\infty Q^\epsilon.
 \label{eq:12}$$ The constant is independent of the prime set, the interval and its location, the function, and the subfamily.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lift-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-lift-moments</summary>

*Proof.* The zero function is immediate; otherwise scale so that $\|f\|_\infty\le1$. Write $N_I=|I|$, and let $u$ be the largest cardinality of a support whose prime product is at most $Q$. We first control conditional sums of squares of the $f_B$, then their square-function moments. Symmetrization will recover the moment of their sum.

<a id="eq:13"></a>
*Conditional energy.* For $J\subseteq\mathcal P$ with $q_J\le Q$, and any fixed coordinate vector $z_J$, we claim that $$\sum_{\substack{B\in\mathcal F\\B\supseteq J}}
       \mathbb E\bigl(|f_B|^2\mid z_J\bigr)\ll4^{|J|}.
 \label{eq:13}$$ The conditional expectation here averages uniformly over all coordinates outside $J$. If $B\supseteq J$, the coefficient of an outside character $\eta$ of exact support $B\setminus J$, after fixing $z_J$, is $$c_J(\eta;z_J)
 =\frac1{N_I}\sum_{x\in I}f(x)e(-x\eta)
       \prod_{p\in J}\bigl(p\mathbf 1_{\{x\equiv z_p\pmod p\}}-1\bigr).$$ Indeed, the sum over the nonzero frequencies at $p$ is $\sum_{a=1}^{p-1}e(a(z_p-x)/p)
=p\mathbf 1_{\{x\equiv z_p\pmod p\}}-1$. Different supports $B\supseteq J$ have disjoint sets of outside characters. Conditional Parseval therefore identifies the left side of <a href="#eq:13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:13">[eq:13]</a> with the squared Euclidean norm of this coefficient vector on the union of those character sets.

Expand the product in $c_J$ over subsets $J'\subseteq J$. Apart from a sign, the corresponding vector has coordinates $$\frac{q_{J'}}{N_I}
 \sum_{\substack{x\in I\\x\equiv z_{J'}\pmod{q_{J'}}}}
                   f(x)e(-x\eta).$$ The congruence denotes the class determined by the fixed coordinates in $J'$; for the empty subset it imposes no restriction. Write its progression as $x=a+q_{J'}n$, with $n$ in a consecutive interval of length $N'$. Then $$N'\ge0.9N_I/q_{J'},
 \qquad N'q_{J'}/N_I\le1.1.$$ In these coordinates the frequencies are $q_{J'}\eta$, up to constant phases. They remain distinct because their denominators are coprime to $q_J$. Their denominators are at most $Q$, so they number at most $Q^2$ and have pairwise circle distance at least $Q^{-2}$.

The Gram matrix of these characters in normalized $L^2$ on the progression has diagonal 1. By geometric summation, every off-diagonal entry has modulus at most $Q^2/(2N')$. Its operator norm is therefore at most its maximum absolute row sum, which is bounded by $$1+\frac{Q^4}{2N'}
 \le1+\frac{Q^5}{1.8N_I}
 \le1+\frac1{18Q}.$$ The associated Fourier analysis map has norm squared equal to this Gram-matrix norm. Since $\|f\|_\infty\le1$ and $N'q_{J'}/N_I\le1.1$, the displayed coefficient vector has Euclidean norm bounded by an absolute constant. The triangle inequality over the $2^{|J|}$ subsets $J'$, followed by squaring, proves <a href="#eq:13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:13">[eq:13]</a>.

<a id="lift:square-function"></a>
*Square-function moments.* Set $$S(z)=\left(\sum_{B\in\mathcal F}|f_B(z)|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Expand $\mathbb ES^{2r}$ and fix the supports of its first $r-1$ factors. Their union $W'$ has size at most $(r-1)u$, and their product depends only on $z_{W'}$. For the final support $B$, integration outside $W'$ leaves exactly $\mathbb E(|f_B|^2\mid z_J)$, where $J=B\cap W'$. Every intersection that occurs satisfies $q_J\le q_B\le Q$. For each fixed intersection $J$, the sum over supports with $B\cap W'=J$ is bounded by the larger sum over all $B\supseteq J$ in <a href="#eq:13" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:13">[eq:13]</a>. There are at most $2^{(r-1)u}$ possible intersections and each has at most $u$ elements. Thus $$\mathbb ES^{2r}\le C\,2^{(r-1)u}4^u\mathbb ES^{2r-2}.$$ The same argument with $r=1$ uses $J=W'=\varnothing$. Iteration gives $$\|S\|_{2r}\le C_r^{1+u}.
 \label{lift:square-function}$$ The overlaps among supports have now been controlled. It remains to bound their sum by this square function with a loss exponential only in $u$.

*Recovering the sum.* Fix a support size $j\ge1$ and color every prime coordinate with one of $j$ colors. Call a support *transversal* when it has exactly one prime of each color, and let $\mathcal T$ be the transversal supports in $\mathcal F$ for this coloring. Take independent uniform pairs $X_p^0,X_p^1$ at every prime. The operator $\operatorname{swap}_p$ interchanges these two copies. Define $$D_B=\prod_{p\in B}(1-\operatorname{swap}_p)f_B(X^0),
 \qquad B\in\mathcal T.$$ Averaging over all second copies leaves $f_B(X^0)$, since every other term in this expansion has mean zero in at least one copied coordinate. Jensen’s inequality gives $$\left\|\sum_{B\in\mathcal T}f_B\right\|_{2r}
 \le\left\|\sum_{B\in\mathcal T}D_B\right\|_{L^{2r}(X^0,X^1)}.$$ Independent swaps preserve the joint law of the copies and multiply $D_B$ by the product of the corresponding signs. Thus for independent uniform signs $\epsilon_p$, indexed by primes, $$\left\|\sum_{B\in\mathcal T}D_B\right\|_{L^{2r}(X^0,X^1)}
 =\left\|\sum_{B\in\mathcal T}D_B\prod_{p\in B}\epsilon_p
       \right\|_{L^{2r}(X^0,X^1,\epsilon)}.$$

For completeness, the scalar sign estimate $$\left\|\sum_i a_i\epsilon_i\right\|_{2r}
 \le C_r\left(\sum_i|a_i|^2\right)^{1/2}$$ follows by expanding the even moment. Surviving terms have even multiplicity at every index. For nonnegative coefficients their sum is at most $$\frac{(2r)!}{2^r r!}\left(\sum_i a_i^2\right)^r,$$ because $(2m)!\ge2^m m!$. For complex coefficients, take absolute values of the surviving terms in $(\sum_i a_i\epsilon_i)^r(\sum_i\overline{a_i}\epsilon_i)^r$ and use the nonnegative case. Iterating this estimate over the color classes gives, for fixed copies, $$\left\|\sum_{B\in\mathcal T}D_B\prod_{p\in B}\epsilon_p
       \right\|_{L^{2r}(\epsilon)}
 \le C_r^j\left(\sum_{B\in\mathcal T}|D_B|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ At each step Minkowski is used in the form $\| (\sum_i|B_i|^2)^{1/2}\|_{2r}
\le(\sum_i\|B_i\|_{2r}^2)^{1/2}$. The signs are indexed by individual primes; transversality ensures that each monomial uses exactly one sign from each color class.

In expanding the swaps in $D_B$, index its $2^j$ terms by a choice of copy for each color. For any fixed such choice, made consistently across all supports, the selected prime coordinates have the original product law. The triangle inequality in $L^{2r}(\ell^2(\mathcal T))$ therefore gives $$\left\|\left(\sum_{B\in\mathcal T}|D_B|^2\right)^{1/2}\right\|_{2r}
 \le2^j\left\|\left(\sum_{B\in\mathcal T}|f_B|^2\right)^{1/2}
       \right\|_{2r}
 \le2^j\|S\|_{2r}.$$ Consequently the transversal sum has norm at most $(2C_r)^j\|S\|_{2r}$.

Now color independently and uniformly. A fixed size-$j$ support is transversal with probability $j!/j^j\ge e^{-j}$, where here $e$ is Euler’s number; the inequality follows from $\log(j!)\ge\int_1^j\log x\,dx$. Thus the full size-$j$ sum is exactly the coloring average of its transversal sum divided by this probability. Minkowski yields $$\left\|\sum_{\substack{B\in\mathcal F\\|B|=j}}f_B\right\|_{2r}
 \le(2eC_r)^j\|S\|_{2r}.$$ Include the constant term of size zero and sum over $j\le u$. Together with <a href="#lift:square-function" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:square-function">[lift:square-function]</a>, this bounds the norm of the full sum by $(C'_r)^{1+u}$. Finally, the product of $u$ distinct primes is at least $(u+1)!$, so $(u+1)!\le Q$. Hence $u=o(\log Q)$ as $Q\to\infty$, uniformly in the prime set. For every fixed $r$ and $\epsilon>0$, $(C'_r)^{1+u}\le C_{r,\epsilon}Q^\epsilon$. Restoring $\|f\|_\infty$ proves <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a> with all the stated uniformities. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lift-specialization"></a>
<div id="lift:specialization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Specialization of residue coordinates). *Let $B\subseteq\mathcal P$, fix $z_B\in X_B$, and let $G:X_{\mathcal P}\to\mathbb C$. For $1\le s<\infty$, $$\|G(z_B,\cdot)\|_{L^s(X_{\mathcal P\setminus B})}
 \le q_B^{1/s}\|G\|_{L^s(X_{\mathcal P})}.$$ In particular, a Fourier lift or a subfamily of its complete supports satisfies <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a> after specialization with the additional factor $q_B^{1/(2r)}$. The same bound holds after retaining either the remaining denominators at most a given $H\ge1$, or those greater than $H$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lift-specialization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lift-specialization</summary>

*Proof.* The specified fiber has uniform probability $1/q_B$. Its contribution to $\mathbb E|G|^s$ is $q_B^{-1}\mathbb E|G(z_B,\cdot)|^s$. Dropping the other nonnegative contributions proves the first assertion. For the last assertion, a character with original exact support $C$ retains exact support $C\setminus B$ after specialization. The two cuts therefore arise, before specialization, from the complete-support subfamilies $$\{C\in\mathcal F:q_{C\setminus B}\le H\},
 \qquad
 \{C\in\mathcal F:q_{C\setminus B}>H\}.$$ Apply Proposition <a href="#lift:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:moments">19</a> to these subfamilies and then use the fiber bound. Combining coefficients can cancel frequencies but cannot create any outside the indicated ranges. ◻

</details>

In particular, applying <a href="#eq:11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:11">[eq:11]</a> with the same set of coordinates fixed in every slot incurs total specialization factor $$q_B^{1/2}\left(q_B^{1/(2(d-1))}\right)^{d-1}=q_B.$$ Two specialized $L^2$ norms incur the same factor. All moment orders needed here are fixed after $h$ is fixed. Finite products of their subpower bounds remain subpower; none of these constants depends on $l$, the specialized residues, or the retained supports.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Parameters and the density quantity

<a id="eq:14"></a>
We now choose the analytic parameters used in the kernel estimate and the scale comparison. The constants $k,d,\gamma$ have already been fixed. Set $$\begin{aligned}
 \eta&=\frac1{100k},&
 \zeta&=\frac{\eta}{20k},&
 \nu&=\frac{\eta}{10\,2^k},\\
 \beta&=\frac{\nu}{100},&
 \tau&=\frac{\beta\gamma}{1000k(d+1)},&
 \sigma&=\frac{\gamma\tau}{4}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:14}$$ The displayed parameters are functions only of the already fixed numbers $k,d,\gamma$. When Condition <a href="#local:unit-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:unit-condition">[local:unit-condition]</a> holds and the designated choices of Sections <a href="#sec:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local">2</a> and <a href="#sec:tuples" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tuples">3</a> are made, these numbers, and hence every parameter in <a href="#eq:14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:14">[eq:14]</a>, depend only on $k$. Here $\eta$ is distinct from the exceptional masses $\eta_{l,p}$. At an integer scale $N\ge1$, the kernel proof will use $\eta$ to set the denominators and widths of neighborhoods of rational frequencies, $\nu$ for the exponential-sum saving $N^{-\nu}$ outside them, and $\zeta$ for the allowed range $l\le N^\zeta$. The exponent $\beta$ fixes the Fourier denominator cutoff $N^\beta$; $\tau$ sets the subdivision into about $N^\tau$ intervals and the bound $q_B\le N^\tau$ in the shorter-scale comparison, while $\sigma$ is the common error exponent in $N^{-\sigma}$. Put $$Q=N^\beta,\qquad H=N^{\beta/2},\qquad
 \mathcal P_N=\{p\text{ prime}:P\le p\le Q\}.$$ For $A\subseteq[N]$, define $$Y_l(N,A)=Z_{l,\mathcal P_N}
             \bigl(\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}\mathbf 1_A\bigr).$$ This is the quantity that will be compared between scales.

<a id="stmt-lift-density"></a>
<div id="lift:density" class="corollary">

**Corollary 21** (Density domination and a uniform upper bound). *For all positive integers $l,N$ and all $A\subseteq[N]$, $$Y_l(N,A)\ge\left(\frac{|A|}{N}\right)^d\ge0.$$ For every $\epsilon>0$, there is a constant $C_\epsilon$, independent of $l,N,A$, such that $$Y_l(N,A)\le C_\epsilon N^\epsilon.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lift-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-lift-density</summary>

*Proof.* The lift is real and has uniform mean $|A|/N$. The lower bound follows from Lemma <a href="#lift:seminorm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:seminorm">17</a>. Ordinary Hölder and the uniform single-slot marginals give $$Y_l(N,A)\le
 \left\|\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}\mathbf 1_A\right\|_d^d.$$ The integer $d$ is even. Also $6\beta<1$, so for all sufficiently large $N$ one has $N\ge10Q^6$. Apply <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a> with moment order $d$, choosing its exponent small enough that its $d$-th power is at most $C_\epsilon N^\epsilon$. In the bounded remaining range of $N$, the lift has a bounded number of coefficients, each of modulus at most one. The probability normalization then supplies a uniform bound independent of $l$, and enlarging $C_\epsilon$ finishes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:kernel"></a>
### A signed kernel for polynomial differences

The tuple laws constrain differences modulo primes. We now connect their pair distributions to actual values of $h_l$ at positive integers. The connection is a uniform Fourier approximation: a kernel supported on those polynomial values will have the same small-denominator multipliers as the pair law, up to a power error. We adapt the signed square-kernel construction of (OpenAI 2026, sec. 6, especially Proposition 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[squarepower](#ref-squarepower)]. For a general polynomial, we realize the pair distribution by weighting regular argument classes; its explicit probability formula is not needed.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Pair multipliers and the ordered estimate

<a id="eq:15"></a>
Fix an integer $l\ge1$, put $F=h_l$, and choose a directed edge $v_j\to v_{j+1}$ of the designated cycle. For $p\ge P$, let $\pi_p$ be the distribution of $z_{v_{j+1}}-z_{v_j}$ under $\mu_{l,p}^{\circ}$. Write $\pi_p(y)$ for its point probabilities and define $$\Gamma_p(a'/p)=\sum_{y\in\mathbb F_p}\pi_p(y)e(a'y/p).$$ The distribution is supported on values of $F$ at regular arguments. Conditioning the character of the second endpoint on the other slots, then pairing with the opposite character of the first endpoint, gives $$|\Gamma_p(a'/p)|\le p^{-\gamma}\qquad(p\nmid a')$$ by <a href="#eq:8" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:8">[eq:8]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz. For a rational $\xi$ with squarefree denominator supported on primes at least $P$, decompose $\xi$ additively into its prime-denominator parts and define $\Gamma(\xi)$ as the product of the corresponding $\Gamma_p$. In particular, $$\Gamma(0)=1,\qquad |\Gamma(\xi)|\le q(\xi)^{-\gamma}.
 \label{eq:15}$$ All estimates below are uniform in the selected cycle edge.

<a id="stmt-kernel-estimate"></a>
<div id="kernel:estimate" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:16"></a>
**Proposition 22** (Ordered polynomial-kernel estimate). *Use the fixed parameters in <a href="#eq:14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:14">[eq:14]</a>, and let $N$ be a sufficiently large integer. Suppose $1\le l\le N^\zeta$ and $$D=M_0\prod_{p\in B}p\le N^\beta,$$ where $B$ is a finite set of primes at least $P$. Let $J_1,J_2:[N]\to\mathbb C$ satisfy $|J_1|,|J_2|\le1$ and be supported in residue classes $a_1,a_2$ modulo $D$, respectively. Suppose that $r=a_2-a_1$ is admissible for $l$ modulo $D$, that $x<y$ whenever $x\in\operatorname{supp}J_1$ and $y\in\operatorname{supp}J_2$, and that no such difference $y-x$ belongs to $F(\mathbb N_+)$. Put $H=N^{\beta/2}$ and $$\Xi=\{\xi\in\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z:q(\xi)\le H,\quad q(\xi)\text{ squarefree},
                         \quad(q(\xi),D)=1\}.$$ Then $$D\left|\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\Gamma(\xi)
          \widehat{J_1}_{[N]}(-\xi)\widehat{J_2}_{[N]}(\xi)\right|
 \ll H^{-\gamma/2}.
 \label{eq:16}$$ The implied constant and the threshold for $N$ depend only on the fixed polynomial and the fixed parameters, not on $l,D,r,J_1,J_2$.*

</div>

Every prime below $P$ divides $D$, so $\Gamma$ is defined at every frequency in $\Xi$. We prove the proposition by constructing two kernels: one encodes its Fourier expression, and the other is supported on forbidden positive polynomial shifts. We first establish the uniform Fourier comparison; a bilinear identity then gives <a href="#eq:16" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:16">[eq:16]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The model kernel and a kernel on polynomial values

Fix the data of Proposition <a href="#kernel:estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kernel:estimate">22</a>. Define the model kernel on $\mathbb Z$ by $$k_0(n)=\frac DN\mathbf 1_{\{1\le n\le N\}}\mathbf 1_{\{n\equiv r\ (D)\}}
                   \sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\Gamma(\xi)e(-\xi n).$$ We first choose periodic argument weights that will reproduce its coefficients. For every $p\mid D$, admissibility and the lifting construction in Section <a href="#sec:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local">2</a> provide an integer $x_p$ with $F(x_p)\equiv r\pmod{p^{E_p}}$ and $s_p=v_p(F'(x_p))$ satisfying $E_p\ge2s_p+1$. On the argument class $x_p\pmod{p^{E_p-s_p}}$, the polynomial $F$ is constant $r$ modulo $p^{E_p}$ and uniform among its higher lifts. Combine these classes by the Chinese remainder theorem. Their modulus $d_r=\prod_{p\mid D}p^{E_p-s_p}$ divides $D$; let $\rho_D$ be $d_r$ times the indicator of the resulting argument class. Thus $\rho_D$ has periodic mean one and supremum at most $D$.

For $p\nmid D$ (and hence $p\ge P$), choose, for each $y$ with $\pi_p(y)>0$, one regular argument $x_y$ satisfying $F(x_y)=y$ in $\mathbb F_p$. Define $$\psi_p(x_y)=p\pi_p(y),\qquad \psi_p(x)=0
       \quad\text{at the other arguments}.$$ Then $\psi_p$ is nonnegative, has uniform mean one, and pushes forward under $F$ to $\pi_p$. In particular $0\le\psi_p\le p$. For positive squarefree integers $u$ coprime to $D$, define periodic functions $$B_p(m)=\psi_p(m)-1,\qquad
 B_u(m)=\prod_{p\mid u}B_p(m),\qquad B_1=1.$$ Each $B_p$ has mean zero, and $|B_u|\le u$. We will use the truncated sum of these products to select the prescribed local distributions at small denominators. Indeed, for positive squarefree $R$ coprime to $D$, $$\sum_{u\mid R}B_u(m)=\prod_{p\mid R}(1+B_p(m))
                    =\prod_{p\mid R}\psi_p(m).$$ The cutoff $u\le H$ preserves this identity when $R\le H$, and we will use local exponential-sum cancellation for larger denominators. The truncated sum may have either sign.

<a id="eq:17"></a>
All coefficients of $F$ are $O_h(a_l)$. Hence one integer $x_*\ge1$, depending only on $h$, can be chosen for the entire auxiliary family, independently of $l$, so that for $x\ge x_*$, $$F(x)\asymp a_lx^k,\qquad F'(x)\asymp a_lx^{k-1},\qquad
 |F''(x)|\ll a_lx^{k-2},$$ with $F'(x)>0$. The lower-degree terms have total absolute value $O_h(a_lx^{k-1})$ for $x\ge1$. Enlarging this same $x_*$ therefore also arranges $$F(x)\ge \frac{a_lx^k}{2}>0\qquad(x\ge x_*),$$ uniformly in $l$. Put $U=(N/a_l)^{1/k}$. Since $k\zeta=\eta/20$, the hypothesis $l\le N^\zeta$ gives $$k!a_l\le k!a l^k\le k!a N^{\eta/20}.$$ The exponent gap $\eta/8-\eta/20=3\eta/40$ is positive. Thus one sufficiently large threshold for $N$, depending on $h$ and the fixed parameters but independent of $l$, ensures $k!a\le N^{3\eta/40}$ and hence, simultaneously for all $l\le N^\zeta$, $$k!a_l\le N^{\eta/8},\qquad
 N^{1-\eta/8}\le U^k\le N.$$ The last inequality uses that $a_l$ is a positive integer. The lower bound for $U$ tends to infinity uniformly in this range of $l$, so increasing the same threshold ensures that there is a unique real $X>x_*$ with $F(X)=N$, and $X\asymp U$. The lower bound on $F(X)$ gives $$X\le (2N/a_l)^{1/k}=2^{1/k}U<2U.$$ With $m$ ranging over integers and $u$ over positive integers, define $$k_1(n)=\sum_{x_*<m\le X}\mathbf 1_{\{n=F(m)\}}\frac{F'(m)}N\rho_D(m)
             \sum_{\substack{u\le H\ \mathrm{squarefree}\\(u,D)=1}}B_u(m).
 \label{eq:17}$$ This kernel is supported on positive values in $F(\mathbb N_+)$. Derivative weights also occur in Lucier’s polynomial-difference argument (Lucier 2006, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Lucier2006](#ref-Lucier2006)]. Here the factor $F'(m)/N$ is chosen so that the corresponding continuous measure becomes $dt/N$ under $t=F(x)$; it will therefore match the interval average in the model kernel.

Use positive-sign transforms for the kernels, and write $$S_i(\alpha)=\sum_n k_i(n)e(\alpha n),\qquad
 V_N(y)=\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N e(yn).$$ Our main analytic claim is the following.

<a id="stmt-kernel-comparison"></a>
<div id="kernel:comparison" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Uniform kernel comparison). *For the kernels just constructed, <a id="eq:18"></a>$$\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}|S_1(\alpha)-S_0(\alpha)|
       \ll H^{-\gamma/2},
 \label{eq:18}$$ uniformly in the data of Proposition <a href="#kernel:estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kernel:estimate">22</a>.*

</div>

<a id="eq:19"></a>
To describe the model transform, expand its congruence indicator: $$S_0(\alpha)=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\sum_{j\bmod D}
           \Gamma(\xi)e(-jr/D)V_N(\alpha-\xi+j/D).
 \label{eq:19}$$ There are at most $DH^2$ terms, all with coefficients of modulus at most one. Their centers $\xi-j/D$ have denominators at most $DH$ and are distinct: equality of two centers makes $\xi-\xi'$ have denominator both coprime to $D$ and dividing $D$, so it is zero.

<a id="eq:20"></a>
Every $\alpha_0\in\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$ has a unique decomposition $\alpha_0=\alpha_D+\alpha_1$ in which $q(\alpha_D)$ has only prime factors dividing $D$ and $(q(\alpha_1),D)=1$. Prime powers are allowed in both parts. The coefficient of the center $\alpha_0$ in <a href="#eq:19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:19">[eq:19]</a>, taken to be zero when that center is absent, is $$c_0(\alpha_0)=
 \begin{cases}
 e(\alpha_Dr)\Gamma(\alpha_1),&q(\alpha_D)\mid D,
                                      \quad\alpha_1\in\Xi,\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:20}$$ Indeed, at a center one has $\alpha_1=\xi$ and $\alpha_D=-j/D$.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Minor arcs: a uniform Weyl estimate

Take the major arcs to be the closed balls of radius $3N^{-1+\eta}$ about the rational points with reduced denominator at most $N^\eta$. They are disjoint for large $N$, since $3\eta<1$. Their complement is the set of minor arcs. We write $\|t\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}$ for distance to the nearest integer. The periodic weights in $k_1$ will introduce linear twists, so the required exponential-sum estimate includes them.

<a id="stmt-kernel-weyl"></a>
<div id="kernel:weyl" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:21"></a>
**Lemma 24**. *If $\alpha$ lies on the minor arcs, then, for every real $y'$ and every integer interval $I\subseteq(x_*,X]$, $$\left|\sum_{m\in I}e\bigl(\alpha F(m)+y'm\bigr)\right|
       \ll U N^{-\nu}.
 \label{eq:21}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-kernel-weyl"><summary>Proof of stmt-kernel-weyl</summary>

*Proof.* Put $A_l=k!a_l$, $\theta'=A_l\alpha$, and $Q_0=\lfloor N^{1-\eta}\rfloor$. Pigeonholing the $Q_0+1$ points $0,\theta',\ldots,Q_0\theta'$ into $Q_0$ equal intervals gives a reduced rational $a'/q'$ such that $$1\le q'\le Q_0,\qquad
 \|\theta'-a'/q'\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}\le\frac1{q'Q_0}\le\frac1{(q')^2}.$$ Lifting this approximation through multiplication by $A_l$ places $\alpha$ within $1/(A_lq'Q_0)$ of a rational with denominator at most $A_lq'$. If $A_lq'\le N^\eta$, this would place $\alpha$ in a major arc, since $Q_0^{-1}\le2N^{-1+\eta}$. Therefore $A_lq'>N^\eta$, and in particular $$N^{\eta/2}\le q'\le N^{1-\eta},$$ using $A_l\le N^{\eta/8}$.

<a id="eq:22"></a>
Choose $M=\lceil2U\rceil$. Since $X<2U$, this integer is larger than all the interval lengths under consideration. Also $U\ge1$, so the absolute comparison $2U\le M\le2U+1\le3U$ holds. Repeated differencing gives $$\begin{split}
 \left|\sum_{m\in I}e\bigl(\alpha F(m)+y'm\bigr)\right|^{2^{k-1}}
 &\le C_k M^{2^{k-1}-k}\\
 &\quad\times\sum_{|b_1|,\ldots,|b_{k-1}|<M}
 \min\!\left(M,\|\theta'b_1\cdots b_{k-1}\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}^{-1}\right).
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:22}$$ Here the minimum equals $M$ when the distance is zero. To verify the estimate, square the sum and group pairs by their difference. After taking absolute values, square again and use Cauchy–Schwarz on the increment indices. At each step the inner sum is over an intersection of interval translates, hence an interval. After $s$ steps the prefactor is $M^{2^s-s-1}$: its exponent is zero for $s=1$, and the next step doubles that exponent and adds $s$. After $k-1$ steps the phase is linear with coefficient $k!a_l\alpha b_1\cdots b_{k-1}$, and a geometric sum proves <a href="#eq:22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:22">[eq:22]</a>. The added linear term contributes only a constant after the first differencing and does not change the estimate. This derivation uses only $|I|\le M$, so also covers short intervals.

The tuples with a zero increment contribute $O_k(M^{k-1})$ inside the sum in <a href="#eq:22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:22">[eq:22]</a>. For the others, group by the absolute product $m\le M^{k-1}$. For each $\epsilon>0$ their multiplicity is $O_{\epsilon,k}(m^\epsilon)$. Indeed, at a prime power $p^a$ the number of exponent choices is a binomial coefficient of fixed degree in $a$; it is at most $p^{a\epsilon}$ for all sufficiently large $p$. The finitely many smaller primes contribute a bounded extra factor, since a fixed polynomial in $a$ divided by $p^{a\epsilon}$ is bounded.

Partition the positive product indices into blocks of $\lfloor q'/2\rfloor$ consecutive integers. If $0<b<q'/2$, then $$\|\theta'b\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}
 \ge\|a'b/q'\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}-\frac b{(q')^2}
 \ge\frac1{2q'}.$$ Thus the points in a block have spacing at least $1/(2q')$ on the circle. Ordering their distances to zero and summing the reciprocal-distance bound gives $O(M+q'\log(2q'))$ per block. There are $O(M^{k-1}/q'+1)$ blocks. Dividing <a href="#eq:22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:22">[eq:22]</a> by $M^{2^{k-1}}$, and absorbing the divisor bound and logarithms into $N^\epsilon$, gives $$C_{\epsilon,k}N^\epsilon
       \left(\frac1{q'}+\frac1M+\frac{q'}{M^k}\right).$$ Here $M^k\ll N$ and $M^k\gg N^{1-\eta/8}$. The three terms are bounded respectively by $N^{-\eta/2}$, $O(N^{-(1-\eta/8)/k})$, and $O(N^{-7\eta/8})$. For sufficiently small fixed $\epsilon$, the displayed expression is $O(N^{-\eta/4})$. Taking its $2^{k-1}$-st root proves <a href="#eq:21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:21">[eq:21]</a>, because $\eta/(4\cdot2^{k-1})=5\nu$. ◻

</details>

We can now bound both kernels on the minor arcs. The supremum and total variation of $F'/N$ on $[x_*,X]$ are $O(1/U)$, so partial summation in <a href="#eq:21" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:21">[eq:21]</a> gives $O(N^{-\nu})$ with this weight. With Fourier coefficients normalized by their period, $\rho_D$ has Fourier $\ell^1$ norm $d_r\le D$. The corresponding norm of $B_u$ is at most $u^2$, by its period and supremum. Expanding these periodic weights into linear twists and summing over $u\le H$ yields $$|S_1(\alpha)|\ll DH^3N^{-\nu}.$$ Every center in <a href="#eq:19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:19">[eq:19]</a> has denominator at most $DH\le N^{3\beta/2}\le N^\eta$. On the minor arcs its distance from $\alpha$ is at least $3N^{-1+\eta}$. The geometric-sum bound $|V_N(t)|\le\min(1,(2N\|t\|_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z})^{-1})$ therefore gives $$|S_0(\alpha)|\ll DH^2N^{-\eta}.$$ These bounds prove the required comparison away from the major arcs. Near a small rational, cancellation in the argument variable must be replaced by an exact calculation of its periodic mean.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Major arcs and the local coefficient calculation

Write $\alpha=\alpha_0+y$, where $q=q(\alpha_0)\le N^\eta$ and $|y|\le3N^{-1+\eta}$. A model center other than $\alpha_0$ is at distance at least $1/(qDH)$ from $\alpha_0$. Since $|y|qDH=o(1)$ uniformly, <a href="#eq:19" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:19">[eq:19]</a> gives $$S_0(\alpha)=c_0(\alpha_0)V_N(y)+O(qD^2H^3/N).$$

<a id="eq:23"></a>
For squarefree $u\le H$ coprime to $D$, let $A_u$ be the periodic mean of $$b_u(m)=\rho_D(m)B_u(m)e(\alpha_0F(m)).$$ Its period is at most $qDu$ and its supremum at most $Du$. Consequently its sum over any real interval differs from $A_u$ times the interval’s length by $O(qD^2u^2)$. For $w(x)=F'(x)e(yF(x))/N$, differentiation gives $$\|w\|_\infty+\int_{x_*}^{X}|w'(x)|\,dx
 \ll\frac{1+|y|N}{U}.$$ Partial summation, followed by the substitution $t=F(x)$, gives $$\sum_{x_*<m\le X}\frac{F'(m)}N e(yF(m))b_u(m)
 =\frac{A_u}{N}\int_{F(x_*)}^{N}e(yt)\,dt
    +O\!\left(qD^2u^2\frac{1+|y|N}{U}\right).$$ The normalized integral differs from $V_N(y)$ by $O(a_l/N+(1+|y|N)/N)$. Multiplying by $|A_u|\le Du$ leaves an error covered by the one already displayed, since $a_l/N=U^{-k}$ and $U\le N$. Summing over $u$ therefore proves $$\begin{split}
 S_1(\alpha)&=c(\alpha_0)V_N(y)
       +O\!\left(qD^2H^3\frac{1+|y|N}{U}\right),\\
 c(\alpha_0)&=\sum_{\substack{u\le H\ \mathrm{squarefree}\\(u,D)=1}}A_u.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:23}$$ The analytic approximations now have the same factor $V_N(y)$. It remains to compare their coefficients. This is where the regular argument weights $\psi_p$ reproduce the pair law. The periodic means $A_u$ and their sum $c(\alpha_0)$ are defined by the same formulas for every rational $\alpha_0$, without a denominator restriction; we use that domain in the next lemma.

<a id="stmt-kernel-coefficients"></a>
<div id="kernel:coefficients" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Comparison of rational coefficients). *For every $\alpha_0\in\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$, write $\alpha_0=\alpha_D+\alpha_1$ for the unique decomposition in which $q(\alpha_D)$ has only prime factors dividing $D$ and $(q(\alpha_1),D)=1$. Then the coefficients defined by <a href="#eq:20" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:20">[eq:20]</a> and <a href="#eq:23" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:23">[eq:23]</a> satisfy $$|c(\alpha_0)-c_0(\alpha_0)|\le H^{-\gamma/2}.$$ In fact they agree when $q(\alpha_1)\le H$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-kernel-coefficients"><summary>Proof of stmt-kernel-coefficients</summary>

*Proof.* In a periodic mean we may average at any common multiple of the periods and separate prime-power factors by the Chinese remainder theorem. First consider a prime $p\mid D$, and abbreviate $E=E_p$, $s=s_p$. On the chosen argument class, Section <a href="#sec:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local">2</a> gives $$F(x_p+p^{E-s}z)=F(x_p)+p^E(c_1z+pC(z)),\qquad p\nmid c_1,$$ where $C$ has integer coefficients. The polynomial in parentheses is a bijection modulo every power of $p$: its difference at two arguments is their difference times a unit. Thus for a local frequency of denominator $p^j$ with $j\le E$, the phase is constant at its value on $r$. This remains true if $j>E-s$. For $j>E$, uniform arguments in the restricted class modulo $p^j$ give uniform $z$ modulo $p^{j-E+s}$, hence uniform reduction modulo $p^{j-E}$. The $F$-values are consequently uniform among the lifts of $r$ modulo $p^j$, and a primitive character has mean zero. The joint factor from primes dividing $D$ is therefore $$\chi_D(\alpha_0)=
 \begin{cases}
 e(\alpha_Dr),&q(\alpha_D)\mid D,\\
 0,&q(\alpha_D)\nmid D.
 \end{cases}$$

<a id="kernel:truncated-coefficient"></a>
Put $q_1=q(\alpha_1)$. If $u$ has a prime not dividing $q_1$, its factor $B_p$ has mean zero independently of all the other factors, so $A_u=0$. Thus only divisors of $\operatorname{rad}(q_1)=\prod_{p\mid q_1}p$ contribute. For $p^j\Vert q_1$, let $\alpha_p$ be the primitive $p^j$-denominator part of $\alpha_1$ and set $$a_{p,j}=\mathbb E_{m\bmod p^j}e(\alpha_pF(m)),\qquad
 b_{p,j}=\mathbb E_{m\bmod p^j}B_p(m)e(\alpha_pF(m)).$$ The Chinese remainder theorem now expresses the truncated coefficient as $$c(\alpha_0)=\chi_D(\alpha_0)
 \sum_{\substack{u\mid\operatorname{rad}(q_1)\\u\le H}}
 \left(\prod_{\substack{p^j\Vert q_1\\p\mid u}}b_{p,j}\right)
 \left(\prod_{\substack{p^j\Vert q_1\\p\nmid u}}a_{p,j}\right).
 \label{kernel:truncated-coefficient}$$ The mean against $1+B_p=\psi_p$ is $$a_{p,j}+b_{p,j}=
 \begin{cases}
 \Gamma_p(\alpha_p),&j=1,\\
 0,&j\ge2.
 \end{cases}$$ For $j=1$ this is the defining pushforward of $\psi_p$. For $j\ge2$, condition on a regular argument modulo $p$. Its lifts are mapped uniformly to the lifts of its value, so their primitive character mean vanishes. This holds on each argument class separately, regardless of the size of $\psi_p$ there.

If $q_1\le H$, all divisors of $\operatorname{rad}(q_1)$ occur. Thus <a href="#kernel:truncated-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kernel:truncated-coefficient">[kernel:truncated-coefficient]</a> factors as $$c(\alpha_0)=\chi_D(\alpha_0)
                    \prod_{p^j\Vert q_1}(a_{p,j}+b_{p,j}).$$ For squarefree $q_1$ this is $\chi_D(\alpha_0)\Gamma(\alpha_1)$; if any exponent is at least two, it is zero. These are precisely the cases in <a href="#eq:20" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:20">[eq:20]</a>. The empty product, when $q_1=1$, also gives the required coefficient.

Suppose instead that $q_1>H$. The model coefficient is then zero. By <a href="#eq:2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:2">[eq:2]</a>, $|a_{p,j}|\le p^{-8\delta j}$. For $j=1$ the preceding identity and <a href="#eq:15" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:15">[eq:15]</a> give $|b_{p,1}|\le2p^{-\gamma}$; for $j\ge2$ the identity gives $b_{p,j}=-a_{p,j}$, hence again $|b_{p,j}|\le2p^{-\gamma j}$. Taking absolute values in <a href="#kernel:truncated-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="kernel:truncated-coefficient">[kernel:truncated-coefficient]</a> and dropping the divisor cutoff yields $$\begin{aligned}
 |c(\alpha_0)|
 &\le\prod_{p^j\Vert q_1}(|a_{p,j}|+|b_{p,j}|)\\
 &\le3^{\omega(q_1)}q_1^{-\gamma}
 \le q_1^{-\gamma/2}<H^{-\gamma/2}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\omega(q_1)$ is the number of distinct prime divisors, and the last inequality before the strict one follows from <a href="#eq:9" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:9">[eq:9]</a>, since every prime of $q_1$ is at least $P$. This bound also covers $\operatorname{rad}(q_1)\le H<q_1$: in that case the full divisor product is present and is actually zero, because some exponent is at least two. If $q(\alpha_D)\nmid D$, all coefficients vanish already through $\chi_D(\alpha_0)$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-kernel-coefficients-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-kernel-coefficients</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#kernel:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kernel:comparison">23</a>.* On the minor arcs the two bounds already proved give $$|S_1|\ll N^{-\nu+(5/2)\beta},\qquad
 |S_0|\ll N^{-\eta+2\beta}.$$ On a major arc, the error terms in the two approximations are at most $$O(N^{\eta+4\beta-1}),\qquad
 O\bigl(N^{2\eta+4\beta-(1-\eta/8)/k}\bigr),$$ respectively. Each of these four bounds is $O(N^{-\beta\gamma/4})=O(H^{-\gamma/2})$ by <a href="#eq:14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:14">[eq:14]</a>. Indeed $\nu=100\beta$, $\eta=1/(100k)$ and $\beta=\eta/(1000\cdot2^k)$, so the two major-arc exponents are less than $-0.99$ and $-0.97/k$, respectively; the minor-arc exponents have still larger margins than required. Lemma <a href="#kernel:coefficients" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kernel:coefficients">25</a> and $|V_N(y)|\le1$ now complete the comparison on the major arcs as well. All thresholds used here are uniform under $l\le N^\zeta$ and $D\le N^\beta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### From Fourier comparison to forbidden differences

<details id="proof-stmt-kernel-coefficients-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-kernel-coefficients</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#kernel:estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kernel:estimate">22</a>.* Extend $J_1,J_2$ by zero to $\mathbb Z$, and for either kernel define $$\mathcal B_i=\frac1N\sum_{x,n\in\mathbb Z}k_i(n)J_1(x)J_2(x+n).$$ Let $\widetilde J_i(\alpha)=\sum_xJ_i(x)e(-x\alpha)$ denote the counting-measure Fourier transforms. Expanding the characters and integrating imposes $n+x-y=0$, so $$\mathcal B_i=\frac1N\int_0^1
 S_i(\alpha)\widetilde J_1(-\alpha)\widetilde J_2(\alpha)\,d\alpha.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz, Plancherel and Lemma <a href="#kernel:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kernel:comparison">23</a> give $$|\mathcal B_1-\mathcal B_0|
 \le\frac{\|S_1-S_0\|_\infty}{N}
             \|J_1\|_{\ell^2(\mathbb Z)}\|J_2\|_{\ell^2(\mathbb Z)}
 \ll H^{-\gamma/2},$$ because the two counting-measure norms are at most $\sqrt N$.

Every term in $\mathcal B_1$ uses a positive shift $F(m)$ with $m\in\mathbb N_+$, and hence vanishes by avoidance. Thus $\mathcal B_1=0$, regardless of the signed weights in <a href="#eq:17" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:17">[eq:17]</a>. For a pair in the supports of $J_1,J_2$, ordering gives $1\le y-x\le N-1$ and the residue conditions give $y-x\equiv r\pmod D$. Both indicators in $k_0$ are therefore automatic, and $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal B_0
 &=\frac D{N^2}\sum_{x,y\in[N]}J_1(x)J_2(y)
                    \sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\Gamma(\xi)e(-\xi(y-x))\\
 &=D\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\Gamma(\xi)
                    \widehat{J_1}_{[N]}(-\xi)\widehat{J_2}_{[N]}(\xi).
\end{aligned}$$ This proves <a href="#eq:16" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:16">[eq:16]</a> with its stated normalization and signs. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:transfer"></a>
### Comparison and cancellation

We now establish the two estimates that will control the quantity $Y_l(N,A)$ in the induction. The first compares a lift, after some prime coordinates have been fixed, with lifts of sets on shorter intervals. The second gives a power saving when the small-prime residues permit every increment of the designated cycle. The progression comparison and the reduction to an ordered pair adapt the arguments of (OpenAI 2026, secs. 7–8, especially Propositions 7.2 and 8.1) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[squarepower](#ref-squarepower)]. For a modulus $D$, our progression spacing is $\lambda(D)$, and the change of variables also changes the auxiliary parameter from $l$ to $lD$.

<a id="eq:24"></a>
For positive integers $l,N$, let $$\mathcal Y_l(N)=\max_A Y_l(N,A),$$ where the maximum is over all $A\subseteq[N]$ satisfying $$\label{eq:24}
 (A-A)\cap h_l(\mathbb N_+)\subseteq\{0\}.$$ We retain the parameters of <a href="#eq:14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:14">[eq:14]</a>, in particular $Q=N^\beta$, $H=N^{\beta/2}$, and $\mathcal P_N=\{p\text{ prime}:P\le p\le Q\}$. For a fixed set $A\subseteq[N]$, define $$f_s(x)=M_0\mathbf 1_{\{x\equiv s\pmod{M_0}\}}\mathbf 1_A(x),
 \qquad
 G_s=\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s,
 \qquad s\in\mathbb Z/M_0\mathbb Z.$$ Thus $\mathbf 1_A$ is the uniform average of the functions $f_s$, and its lift is the corresponding average of the $G_s$.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Fourier coefficients after specialization

Fixing prime coordinates restricts the underlying integers to a residue class, provided the cutoff includes all characters on those coordinates. The precise identity is the following.

<a id="stmt-transfer-specialization"></a>
<div id="transfer:specialization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:25"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Specialization of a lift). *Let $I\subseteq[N]$ be a nonempty integer interval, let $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$, and put $D=M_0q_B$. Fix $s\in\mathbb Z/M_0\mathbb Z$ and $b=(b_p)_{p\in B}\in X_B$. Let $g$ be the function on $X_{\mathcal P_N\setminus B}$ obtained by specializing $\mathcal L_{I,\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s$ at $z_B=b$. For every remaining-coordinate frequency $\xi$ with $q(\xi)q_B\le Q$, one has $$\label{eq:25}
 \widehat g(\xi)
 =\frac{D}{|I|}
   \sum_{\substack{x\in I\\x\equiv a'\pmod D}}
       \mathbf 1_A(x)e(-x\xi),$$ where $a'\pmod D$ is determined by $a'\equiv s\pmod{M_0}$ and $a'\equiv b_p\pmod p$ for $p\in B$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-transfer-specialization"><summary>Proof of stmt-transfer-specialization</summary>

*Proof.* The frequencies contributing to $\widehat g(\xi)$ have the form $\xi+\omega$, where $\omega$ ranges over the characters on $X_B$ that survive the cutoff. The denominators are coprime, so $q(\xi+\omega)=q(\xi)q(\omega)\le q(\xi)q_B\le Q$. Consequently every character on $X_B$ occurs in the specialized coefficient, which equals $$\frac{M_0}{|I|}\sum_{x\in I}
 \mathbf 1_A(x)\mathbf 1_{\{x\equiv s\pmod{M_0}\}}e(-x\xi)
 \sum_{\omega\text{ on }X_B}e((b-x)\omega).$$ Character orthogonality makes the inner sum $q_B$ when $x\equiv b_p\pmod p$ for every $p\in B$, and zero otherwise. The Chinese remainder theorem now gives <a href="#eq:25" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:25">[eq:25]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Comparison with a shorter interval

The next estimate is uniform in the auxiliary parameter $l$. This uniformity is necessary because a progression replaces $l$ by $lD$, which need not satisfy the size restriction used in the kernel estimate.

<a id="stmt-transfer-shorter-scale"></a>
<div id="transfer:shorter-scale" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:26"></a>
<a id="eq:27"></a>
**Proposition 27** (Shorter-scale comparison). *Let $N$ be sufficiently large, let $l\ge1$, and let $A\subseteq[N]$ satisfy <a href="#eq:24" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:24">[eq:24]</a>. Suppose that $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$ and $q_B\le N^\tau$. Set $$\begin{gathered}
 \mathcal S=\mathcal P_N\setminus B,
 \qquad D=M_0q_B,
 \qquad L_D=\lambda(D),\\
 n_B=\left\lceil\frac{N}{L_D}\right\rceil,
 \qquad \Lambda_B=\frac{L_Dn_B}{N}.
\end{gathered}$$ For each $v\in V$, choose $s_v\in\mathbb Z/M_0\mathbb Z$ and specialize $G_{s_v}$ at an arbitrary vector of coordinates on $B$, obtaining a function $g_v$ on $X_{\mathcal S}$. Then $$\label{eq:26}
 \bigl|\mathcal Z_{l,\mathcal S}((g_v)_{v\in V})\bigr|
 \le \Lambda_B^d\mathcal Y_{lD}(n_B)+O(N^{-\sigma}).$$ The scale and the rounding factor satisfy $1\le n_B<N$ and $$\label{eq:27}
 1\le\Lambda_B\le1+M_0^kN^{k\tau-1}.$$ The implied constant and the threshold for $N$ are independent of $l,A,B$ and all specializations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-transfer-shorter-scale"><summary>Proof of stmt-transfer-shorter-scale</summary>

*Proof.* We first record the scale inequalities. By <a href="#eq:1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:1">[eq:1]</a>, $D\le L_D\le D^k$, so $L_D\ge M_0\ge2$ and $n_B<N$ for large $N$. The defining ceiling gives <a href="#eq:27" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:27">[eq:27]</a>. Also $$\begin{gathered}
 n_B\ge M_0^{-k}N^{1-k\tau}\ge N^{1/2},\\
 H\le Q':=n_B^\beta\le Q,
 \qquad q_BH\le Q.
\end{gathered}$$ Here $k\tau<1/2$ and $\tau<\beta/2$ follow from <a href="#eq:14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:14">[eq:14]</a>.

By the signed Hölder inequality <a href="#eq:10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:10">[eq:10]</a>, it is enough to prove the asserted common bound for $Z_{l,\mathcal S}(g)$, where $g$ is any one of the specialized inputs. Let $g_{\le H}$ retain exactly the remaining-coordinate frequencies of denominator at most $H$. We claim that $$Z_{l,\mathcal S}(g)
 =Z_{l,\mathcal S}(g_{\le H})
   +O(H^{-\gamma}q_BN^{o(1)}).$$ To see this, telescope the $d$ slots and apply <a href="#eq:11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:11">[eq:11]</a> to the high-frequency input in each term. The moment estimate <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a> is applicable to all the other inputs as well as to that high-frequency piece. Indeed, before specialization, a cut on the remaining denominator retains entire supports $C\subseteq\mathcal P_N$ according to the condition on $q_{C\setminus B}$; it never selects only part of the frequencies with a given full support $C$. Fixing $B$ costs at most $q_B^{1/2}$ in the distinguished $L^2$ norm and $q_B^{1/[2(d-1)]}$ in each of the other $d-1$ norms. The total cost is therefore $q_B$. The original input is bounded by $M_0$, and $N\ge10Q^6$ for large $N$, so <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a> supplies the remaining $N^{o(1)}$ factor. Since $$\frac{\beta\gamma}{2}-\tau-\sigma
 =\tau\left(500k(d+1)-1-\frac\gamma4\right)>0,$$ the claimed error is $O(N^{-\sigma})$. The denominator cuts are symmetric under negation, so the functions used here are real.

We next express $g_{\le H}$ as an average of shorter lifts. Let $a'\pmod D$ be the residue in Lemma <a href="#transfer:specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:specialization">26</a>. For each representative $j\in[L_D]$ with $j\equiv a'\pmod D$, set $$c_j=j-L_D,\qquad
 A'_j=\{x'\in[n_B]:L_Dx'+c_j\in A\}.$$ These progressions partition the integers of $[N]$ in the class $a'\pmod D$: the first positive term is $j$, and $n_B$ terms suffice for every such integer. Terms above $N$ contribute no points to $A'_j$. Moreover, $A'_j$ satisfies <a href="#eq:24" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:24">[eq:24]</a> with $lD$ in place of $l$. For if a nonzero difference in $A'_j-A'_j$ were $h_{lD}(m)$ with $m\ge1$, the corresponding difference in $A-A$ would be $$L_Dh_{lD}(m)=h_l(Dm-i),\qquad 0\le i<D,$$ by <a href="#eq:1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:1">[eq:1]</a>; its argument is positive and its value is nonzero.

<a id="eq:28"></a>
Put $\mathcal H_j=\mathcal L_{[n_B],\mathcal S,H}\mathbf 1_{A'_j}$. Every prime of $L_D$ divides $D$, so $L_D$ is invertible on $X_{\mathcal S}$ and multiplication by $L_D$ preserves denominators. Lemma <a href="#transfer:specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:specialization">26</a>, applied using $q_BH\le Q$, gives the exact identity $$\label{eq:28}
 g_{\le H}(z)
 =\frac{\Lambda_B}{L_D/D}
   \sum_{\substack{j\in[L_D]\\j\equiv a'\pmod D}}
    \mathcal H_j\bigl(L_D^{-1}(z-c_j)\bigr).$$ For completeness, the summand on the right has Fourier coefficient $\widehat{\mathcal H_j}(L_D\xi)e(-c_j\xi)$ at $\xi$. Since $\Lambda_B/(L_D/D)=Dn_B/N$, summing these coefficients gives $D/N$ times the sum over the required original integers, exactly as in <a href="#eq:25" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:25">[eq:25]</a>; both sides have zero coefficients above $H$.

Under the map $z\mapsto L_D^{-1}(z-c_j)$, the tuple law for $l$ pushes forward to the tuple law for $lD$, by <a href="#eq:7" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:7">[eq:7]</a> at each remaining prime. There are exactly $L_D/D$ representatives $j$. The seminorm triangle inequality applied to <a href="#eq:28" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:28">[eq:28]</a> therefore yields $$Z_{l,\mathcal S}(g_{\le H})
 \le\Lambda_B^d\max_j Z_{lD,\mathcal S}(\mathcal H_j).$$ This is where the exact change of tuple laws preserves the leading constant in the comparison.

It remains to replace the cutoff $H$ on the shorter interval by its natural cutoff $Q'$. Equations <a href="#eq:11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:11">[eq:11]</a> and <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a>, now on $[n_B]$, bound the change of the diagonal by $O(H^{-\gamma}N^{o(1)})=O(N^{-\sigma})$. Their interval hypothesis holds because $6\beta<1$, and hence $n_B\ge10(Q')^6$ for large $N$. The extended lift only uses primes in $\mathcal P_{n_B}$. It is obtained from $\mathcal L_{[n_B],\mathcal P_{n_B},Q'}\mathbf 1_{A'_j}$ by averaging the coordinates in $B\cap\mathcal P_{n_B}$ and then viewing the result as independent of the additional coordinates in $\mathcal S$. The contraction under coordinate averaging from Lemma <a href="#lift:seminorm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:seminorm">17</a> gives $$Z_{lD,\mathcal S}(\mathcal H_j)
 \le Y_{lD}(n_B,A'_j)+O(N^{-\sigma})
 \le\mathcal Y_{lD}(n_B)+O(N^{-\sigma}).$$ Combining these estimates with <a href="#eq:27" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:27">[eq:27]</a> proves the diagonal bound. Applying <a href="#eq:10" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:10">[eq:10]</a> to the mixed inputs proves <a href="#eq:26" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:26">[eq:26]</a>: the product of $d$th roots of quantities bounded by the same nonnegative upper bound is at most that upper bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Cancellation for good residue assignments

The previous comparison applies to every residue assignment. We now use avoidance to obtain a stronger estimate for assignments compatible with the designated cycle. An assignment $(s_v)_{v\in V}\in(\mathbb Z/M_0\mathbb Z)^V$ is *good for $l$* if every increment $s_{v_{j+1}}-s_{v_j}$, $0\le j<L$, is admissible for $l$ modulo $M_0$. As before, $v_L=v_0$.

<a id="stmt-transfer-good-assignments"></a>
<div id="transfer:good-assignments" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28**. *For every $l\ge1$, the proportion $\rho_l$ of good assignments under independent uniform choices of all the $s_v$ satisfies $$\rho_l\ge\rho_*:=M_0^{1-L}>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-transfer-good-assignments"><summary>Proof of stmt-transfer-good-assignments</summary>

*Proof.* At every prime-power factor of $M_0$, the construction in Section <a href="#sec:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local">2</a> supplies a length-$L$ sequence of admissible residues whose sum is zero. The Chinese remainder theorem combines them into such a sequence modulo $M_0$. Fix one sequence. There are $M_0$ choices of the initial cycle residue; the other cycle residues are then determined, and the zero-sum condition closes the cycle. Its $L$ vertices are distinct. The remaining $d-L$ residues can be arbitrary, giving at least $M_0^{d-L+1}$ good assignments out of $M_0^d$. ◻

</details>

The proportion $\rho_l$ may vary with $h$ and $l$. When Condition <a href="#local:unit-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:unit-condition">[local:unit-condition]</a> holds and the designated choices of Sections <a href="#sec:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local">2</a> and <a href="#sec:tuples" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tuples">3</a> are made, both $M_0$ and $L$, and therefore the lower bound $\rho_*=M_0^{1-L}$, depend only on $k$.

<a id="stmt-transfer-cancellation"></a>
<div id="transfer:cancellation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:29"></a>
**Proposition 29** (Cancellation on good assignments). *Let $N$ be sufficiently large, let $l\le N^\zeta$, and let $A\subseteq[N]$ satisfy <a href="#eq:24" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:24">[eq:24]</a>. If $(s_v)_{v\in V}$ is good for $l$, then $$\label{eq:29}
 \left|
  \int\prod_{v\in V}G_{s_v}(z_v)\,
    d\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}
       (\mu_{l,p}-\varepsilon_{l,p})
 \right|\ll N^{-\sigma}.$$ The bound is uniform in $l,A$ and the good assignment.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-transfer-cancellation"><summary>Proof of stmt-transfer-cancellation</summary>

*Proof.* We may replace each $\mu_{l,p}-\varepsilon_{l,p}$ by its normalized law $\mu_{l,p}^{\circ}$: passing back multiplies the integral by $\prod_{p\in\mathcal P_N}(1-\eta_{l,p})\le1$. We first locate an ordered pair of intervals along the cycle. We then truncate the other inputs and condition on their prime supports; the remaining pair integral will have exactly the multipliers appearing in Proposition <a href="#kernel:estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kernel:estimate">22</a>.

Partition $[N]$ into $\lfloor N^\tau\rfloor$ consecutive intervals whose lengths differ by at most one. Each $G_s$ is the average of the interval lifts $\mathcal L_{I,\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s$ with weights $|I|/N$. Thus we may average independently over an interval assignment to every slot. These intervals have length $\gg N^{1-\tau}$, so the moment estimate <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a> applies: indeed $6\beta+\tau<7\beta<1$ by <a href="#eq:14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:14">[eq:14]</a>. For any interval assignment, ordinary Hölder, uniform single-slot marginals and <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a> bound the integral in absolute value by $N^{o(1)}$. The probability that all $L$ cycle slots choose the same interval is $$\sum_I\left(\frac{|I|}{N}\right)^L
 \le\left(\max_I\frac{|I|}{N}\right)^{L-1}
 \ll N^{-(L-1)\tau}.$$ Their contribution is $O(N^{-\sigma})$, since $(L-1)\tau>\sigma=\gamma\tau/4$.

In every other assignment, some directed cycle edge has its first interval $I_1$ strictly before its second interval $I_2$. Otherwise the interval indices would be nonincreasing all the way around the cycle, and therefore constant. Fix such an edge for the current assignment. The constants below are uniform in this choice.

Set $T=N^\tau$ and truncate every interval lift except the two selected ones to denominators at most $T$. Telescoping the other slots, <a href="#eq:11" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:11">[eq:11]</a> and <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a> give total error $O(T^{-\gamma}N^{o(1)})=O(N^{-\sigma})$, because $\gamma\tau>\sigma$. Expand the truncated inputs into characters. Each coefficient has modulus at most $M_0$, and there are at most $T^2$ rational frequencies of denominator at most $T$. Thus the sum of the absolute coefficients in the resulting expansion is $O(T^{2(d-2)})$.

<a id="eq:30"></a>
Fix one term, let $B$ be the union of the supports of its $d-2$ characters, and put $$R=q_B\le T^{d-2},\qquad
 \mathcal S=\mathcal P_N\setminus B.$$ Condition on all tuple labels at every prime in $B$. The expanded characters become constants of modulus one. By independence between primes, the remaining tuple labels still have law $\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal S}\mu_{l,p}^{\circ}$. Let $g_1,g_2$ denote the two selected interval lifts after specialization at this conditioning. They are functions on $X_{\mathcal S}$. For the chosen directed edge, let $\Gamma$ be the increment multiplier defined in Section <a href="#sec:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:kernel">5</a>. The remaining pair integral is exactly $$\label{eq:30}
 \sum_{\xi\text{ on }X_{\mathcal S}}
      \widehat g_1(-\xi)\widehat g_2(\xi)\Gamma(\xi).$$ Indeed, simultaneous translation of the two labels kills all pairs of characters whose frequencies do not sum to zero. The surviving pair with frequencies $-\xi,\xi$ has value $e(\xi(z_2-z_1))$, whose expectation is $\Gamma(\xi)$.

For the terms with $q(\xi)>H$, <a href="#eq:15" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:15">[eq:15]</a> and Parseval give $$\left|\sum_{q(\xi)>H}
      \widehat g_1(-\xi)\widehat g_2(\xi)\Gamma(\xi)\right|
 \le H^{-\gamma}\|g_1\|_2\|g_2\|_2
 \ll H^{-\gamma}R N^{o(1)}.$$ The last inequality uses the interval moment bound and the cost $R^{1/2}$ of each specialization in $L^2$.

We apply the kernel estimate to the remaining terms. Put $D=M_0R$. The parameter choice gives $$(d-2)\tau<\frac\beta2,
 \qquad HR\le Q,
 \qquad D\le N^\beta$$ for large $N$; the first inequality follows directly from $\tau=\beta\gamma/[1000k(d+1)]$. By Lemma <a href="#transfer:specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:specialization">26</a>, their Fourier coefficient formulas for $q(\xi)\le H$ restrict the underlying integers to classes $a'_1,a'_2\pmod D$. The difference $a'_2-a'_1$ is admissible modulo $M_0$ by goodness. It is admissible at each prime in $B$ because every conditioning of positive probability is in the cycle support of the nonexceptional law. It is therefore admissible modulo $D$.

The remaining frequencies of denominator at most $H$ are precisely the set $\Xi$ in Proposition <a href="#kernel:estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kernel:estimate">22</a>. Indeed, their denominators are squarefree and coprime to $D$. Conversely, a squarefree denominator at most $H$ and coprime to $D$ has no prime below $P$, since all those primes divide $M_0$, and no prime in $B$; every prime divisor is at most $H\le Q$.

Define functions on $[N]$ by $$J_i(x)=\mathbf 1_A(x)\mathbf 1_{I_i}(x)
             \mathbf 1_{\{x\equiv a'_i\pmod D\}},\qquad i=1,2.$$ They are bounded by one and have ordered supports. Every difference from the first support to the second is positive, so a zero polynomial value cannot be such a difference; <a href="#eq:24" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:24">[eq:24]</a> excludes all nonzero values of $h_l(\mathbb N_+)$. Thus all hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#kernel:estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="kernel:estimate">22</a> hold at scale $N$. The Fourier normalizations in <a href="#eq:25" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:25">[eq:25]</a> are $$\widehat g_i(\xi)
 =\frac{DN}{|I_i|}\widehat{J_i}_{[N]}(\xi)
 \qquad(q(\xi)\le H).$$ Since <a href="#eq:16" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:16">[eq:16]</a> has a factor $D$ outside its absolute value, the low-denominator part of <a href="#eq:30" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:30">[eq:30]</a> is at most $$C H^{-\gamma/2}D\frac{N^2}{|I_1||I_2|}
 \ll H^{-\gamma/2}D N^{2\tau}.$$

Both parts of <a href="#eq:30" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:30">[eq:30]</a> are consequently bounded, uniformly in the conditioning and in the term of the character expansion, by $$O\!\left(N^{-\beta\gamma/4+(d+2)\tau+o(1)}\right).$$ Averaging the conditioning costs no factor, and summing the character expansion costs at most $O(N^{2d\tau})$. The final exponent satisfies $$\frac{\beta\gamma}{4}-(3d+2)\tau-\sigma
 =\tau\left(250k(d+1)-(3d+2)-\frac\gamma4\right)>0.$$ Thus the resulting error is $O(N^{-\sigma})$. The same-interval contribution and the truncation error already satisfy this bound. Finally, the interval assignments were averaged with probabilities summing to one, so the uniform estimate proves <a href="#eq:29" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:29">[eq:29]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:induction"></a>
### Exceptional terms and the power-saving induction

We now combine the shorter-scale comparison with cancellation on good residue assignments to obtain a recurrence for $\mathcal Y_l(N)$. The exceptional parts of the prime laws contribute a summable family of additional shorter-scale terms. The expansion and contraction argument adapt (OpenAI 2026, Proposition 9.2 and Theorem 9.3) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[squarepower](#ref-squarepower)].

Retain all parameters from <a href="#eq:14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:14">[eq:14]</a>. For $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$, put $$D_B=M_0q_B,\qquad
 n_B=\left\lceil\frac{N}{\lambda(D_B)}\right\rceil,
 \qquad
 \eta_{l,B}=\prod_{p\in B}\eta_{l,p}.$$ Thus $q_\varnothing=\eta_{l,\varnothing}=1$ and $D_\varnothing=M_0$. Recall that $\rho_l$ is the proportion of good residue assignments for $h_l$, and that $\rho_l\ge\rho_*=M_0^{1-L}>0$ by Lemma <a href="#transfer:good-assignments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:good-assignments">28</a>.

<a id="stmt-induction-recurrence"></a>
<div id="induction:recurrence" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:31"></a>
**Proposition 30** (Recurrence for the maximal functional). *There are constants $C_1\ge1$ and $N_1\ge3$, depending only on the fixed polynomial $h$, its chosen roots, and the fixed structural choices, such that for every integer $N\ge N_1$ and every positive integer $l\le N^\zeta$, $$\begin{split}
 \mathcal Y_l(N)\le C_1N^{-\sigma}
 +(1+u_N)\Bigg(&
   (1-\rho_l)\mathcal Y_{lD_\varnothing}(n_\varnothing)\\
 &+\rho_l
   \sum_{\substack{\varnothing\ne B\subseteq\mathcal P_N\\q_B\le N^\tau}}
      \eta_{l,B}\mathcal Y_{lD_B}(n_B)\Bigg),
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:31}$$ where $$u_N=\bigl(1+M_0^kN^{k\tau-1}\bigr)^d-1\longrightarrow0.$$ Every scale $n_B$ appearing on the right belongs to $[1,N-1]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-induction-recurrence"><summary>Proof of stmt-induction-recurrence</summary>

<a id="induction:residue-average"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $A\subseteq[N]$ satisfying <a href="#eq:24" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:24">[eq:24]</a>. Use the functions $f_s$ and their lifts $G_s$ from Section <a href="#sec:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer">6</a>, and, for a residue assignment $\mathbf s=(s_v)_{v\in V}$, write $$\Phi_{\mathbf s}((z_v)_{v\in V})=\prod_{v\in V}G_{s_v}(z_v).$$ Since $\mathbb E_s f_s=\mathbf 1_A$, linearity of the lift and multilinearity of the integral give $$Y_l(N,A)=\mathbb E_{\mathbf s}
   \int\Phi_{\mathbf s}\,d\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}\mu_{l,p},
 \label{induction:residue-average}$$ where the residues $s_v$ are independent and uniform modulo $M_0$.

<a id="induction:exceptional-expansion"></a>
For each $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$, define the nonnegative measure $$\kappa_{l,B}=
   \bigotimes_{p\in B}\varepsilon_{l,p}
   \otimes\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N\setminus B}\mu_{l,p}.$$ Its mass is $\eta_{l,B}$. Expanding the finite product gives the identity of signed measures $$\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}\mu_{l,p}
 -\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}(\mu_{l,p}-\varepsilon_{l,p})
 =\sum_{\varnothing\ne B\subseteq\mathcal P_N}
      (-1)^{|B|+1}\kappa_{l,B}.
 \label{induction:exceptional-expansion}$$ The complement of $B$ therefore carries the full laws $\mu_{l,p}$, to which Proposition <a href="#transfer:shorter-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:shorter-scale">27</a> applies. The functions $\Phi_{\mathbf s}$ can have either sign; we will take the absolute value of each integral on the right of <a href="#induction:exceptional-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="induction:exceptional-expansion">[induction:exceptional-expansion]</a>.

<a id="eq:32"></a>
The exceptional masses have a uniform bound with an extra factor $q_B$: $$\sum_{\varnothing\ne B\subseteq\mathcal P_N}q_B\eta_{l,B}
 =\prod_{p\in\mathcal P_N}(1+p\eta_{l,p})-1
 \le\prod_{p\ge P}(1+p^{-3})-1\le\frac14.
 \label{eq:32}$$ Here we used $\eta_{l,p}\le p^{-4}$ and the choice of $P$ in <a href="#eq:9" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:9">[eq:9]</a>. This bound first allows us to discard the terms with $q_B>N^\tau$.

<a id="induction:exceptional-moment"></a>
To see this, suppose $\eta_{l,B}>0$. Simultaneous translation invariance of $\varepsilon_{l,p}$ implies that each single-label marginal of $\varepsilon_{l,p}/\eta_{l,p}$ is uniform on $\mathbb F_p$. The same holds for the full laws. Thus each single-slot marginal of $\kappa_{l,B}/\eta_{l,B}$ is uniform on $X_{\mathcal P_N}$. Ordinary Hölder’s inequality and the moment estimate <a href="#eq:12" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:12">[eq:12]</a>, at the fixed even exponent $d$, give $$\left|\int\Phi_{\mathbf s}\,d\kappa_{l,B}\right|
 \le\eta_{l,B}\prod_{v\in V}\|G_{s_v}\|_d
 \le C_\epsilon\eta_{l,B}N^\epsilon
 \qquad(\epsilon>0).
 \label{induction:exceptional-moment}$$ The constants are uniform in $l,B,A$ and $\mathbf s$; the bound $|f_s|\le M_0$ only introduces a fixed factor. Terms of zero mass vanish. Combining <a href="#induction:exceptional-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="induction:exceptional-moment">[induction:exceptional-moment]</a> with <a href="#eq:32" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:32">[eq:32]</a> yields $$\sum_{\substack{\varnothing\ne B\subseteq\mathcal P_N\\q_B>N^\tau}}
   \left|\int\Phi_{\mathbf s}\,d\kappa_{l,B}\right|
 \le \frac{C_\epsilon}{4}N^{-\tau+\epsilon}
 =O(N^{-\sigma}),$$ where, for example, $\epsilon=\tau/2$ suffices because $\sigma=\gamma\tau/4<\tau/2$.

<a id="induction:exceptional-transfer"></a>
For $q_B\le N^\tau$ and positive mass, condition instead on all the labels at primes in $B$. The laws at the remaining primes are unchanged, and the function in each slot is the corresponding specialization of $G_{s_v}$. Proposition <a href="#transfer:shorter-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:shorter-scale">27</a>, which allows arbitrary slotwise specializations, gives $$\left|\int\Phi_{\mathbf s}\,d\kappa_{l,B}\right|
 \le\eta_{l,B}\left(
      \Lambda_B^d\mathcal Y_{lD_B}(n_B)+C_2N^{-\sigma}\right),
 \qquad
 \Lambda_B=\frac{\lambda(D_B)n_B}{N}.
 \label{induction:exceptional-transfer}$$ The bound is uniform in every conditioned tuple, so integration over the prescribed labels preserves it. It also holds for zero-mass terms. By <a href="#eq:27" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:27">[eq:27]</a>, $\Lambda_B^d\le1+u_N$; by <a href="#eq:32" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:32">[eq:32]</a>, $\sum_{B\ne\varnothing}\eta_{l,B}\le1/4$. Therefore summing the errors in <a href="#induction:exceptional-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="induction:exceptional-transfer">[induction:exceptional-transfer]</a> still costs $O(N^{-\sigma})$.

For a good assignment $\mathbf s$, Proposition <a href="#transfer:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:cancellation">29</a> bounds the integral against $\bigotimes_p(\mu_{l,p}-\varepsilon_{l,p})$ by $O(N^{-\sigma})$ in absolute value. The expansion <a href="#induction:exceptional-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="induction:exceptional-expansion">[induction:exceptional-expansion]</a> and the two exceptional estimates consequently give $$\int\Phi_{\mathbf s}\,d\bigotimes_p\mu_{l,p}
 \le O(N^{-\sigma})+(1+u_N)
   \sum_{\substack{\varnothing\ne B\subseteq\mathcal P_N\\q_B\le N^\tau}}
     \eta_{l,B}\mathcal Y_{lD_B}(n_B).$$ For every other assignment, apply Proposition <a href="#transfer:shorter-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:shorter-scale">27</a> directly with $B=\varnothing$. The resulting bound is $(1+u_N)\mathcal Y_{lM_0}(n_\varnothing)+O(N^{-\sigma})$. Averaging these estimates in <a href="#induction:residue-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="induction:residue-average">[induction:residue-average]</a> gives the coefficients $\rho_l$ and $1-\rho_l$ in <a href="#eq:31" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:31">[eq:31]</a>. The bounds are uniform in $A$, so we may take the maximum defining $\mathcal Y_l(N)$.

Finally, $k\tau<1$ by <a href="#eq:14" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:14">[eq:14]</a>, so $u_N\to0$, and the bound $1\le n_B<N$ is part of Proposition <a href="#transfer:shorter-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="transfer:shorter-scale">27</a>. ◻

</details>

The recurrence decreases $N$ while replacing $l$ by $lD_B$. Its cancellation hypothesis $l\le N^\zeta$ need not persist at the smaller scale. The next estimate is therefore stated for every $l$: a small positive power of $l$ permits the uniform moment bound to handle the range where that hypothesis fails.

<a id="stmt-induction-decay"></a>
<div id="induction:decay" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:33"></a>
**Proposition 31** (Decay throughout the auxiliary family). *There are constants $a_0,b_0>0$ and $C_0\ge1$, depending only on the fixed polynomial $h$, its chosen roots, and the fixed structural choices, such that for all positive integers $l,N$, $$\mathcal Y_l(N)\le C_0l^{b_0}N^{-a_0}.
 \label{eq:33}$$ The exponents $a_0,b_0$ can be chosen as functions only of the structural parameters $k,\zeta,\sigma,M_0,\rho_*$. If the chosen roots of $h$ satisfy Condition <a href="#local:unit-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:unit-condition">[local:unit-condition]</a> and the choices depending only on $k$ from Sections <a href="#sec:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local">2</a> and <a href="#sec:tuples" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tuples">3</a> are used, then $a_0,b_0,d$ depend only on $k$, while $C_0$ may still depend on the fixed data.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-induction-decay"><summary>Proof of stmt-induction-decay</summary>

*Proof.* To select the exponents, suppose that at a scale where the recurrence applies the trial bound $\mathcal Y_{lD_B}(n_B)\le C(lD_B)^b n_B^{-a}$ is available at every successor scale, with $C\ge1$ and $a,b>0$. Put $s=ka+b$. Since $n_B\ge N/\lambda(D_B)$ and $\lambda(D_B)\le D_B^k$, each trial bound gives $$C(lD_B)^b n_B^{-a}
 \le C l^bN^{-a}\lambda(D_B)^aD_B^b
 \le C l^bN^{-a}D_B^s.$$ If $s\le1$, then $q_B^s\le q_B$, so Equation <a href="#eq:32" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:32">[eq:32]</a> bounds the coefficient of $C l^bN^{-a}$ inside the parentheses of <a href="#eq:31" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:31">[eq:31]</a> by $$M_0^s\left(1-\rho_l+
